The two resolvers keep their interfaces over the JSON scope: `bin/fusion-paths` takes `CONTAINER=` from `bin/fusion-claimed-package`, which FJ03a step 3 puts on `inspect`, `list` and `show`, and `bin/fusion-rules` takes `PACKAGE=` from the same helper. The second half of the row names the active documents, which a package record carries as `active_documents[]` (section 4.2: at most one entry of role `plan`, any number of role `spec`, each pinned to its narrative's revision by `adopt-plan`). Measured at `b4c8f7ca` and re-checked at `4c800f17`: `bin/fusion-paths` emits only the keys a consumer's prompt names (`rules/workbench-path-resolution.md`, the key table), and no agent prompt, no skill body and no file under `bin/` or `hooks/lib/` names a key for an active document. So FJ03a adds no key and reads no active document: a key with no consumer is the case the resolver rule excludes, and the prompts that would consume one are FJ03d's, under the growth bound. Fusion's reading of the row: for the resolvers, "nutzen" is met when scope resolution reads the package record the active documents stand in, which `show` does; a resolver key for the plan in force, if Prior means one, is added in FJ03d together with the prompt that reads it. ### 30. The Claude side's recovery declaration, for confirmation

**Closes:** fusion decision `260929-1810_*_what-does-the-claude-side-declare-about-a-read-that-finishes-a-committed-intent.md` (option 1, answered by `Prior: docs/design/fusion-fj03a-prior-plan-response.md` `## C. Explicit recovery policy; automatic hooks do not invoke the codec` at Prior `b2a931b`), and the sentence in `Prior: docs/design/fusion-fj02-prior-response.md` `## Recovery and rollout consequences` that a host's recovery authority must cover a read finishing a committed intent and that a read-only role must not receive recovery authority from the operation name. Preferred form: a reply, confirm or correct, that the declaration as landed is the declared policy `## C.` accepted.

`## C.` accepted the policy as the plan stated it. What is asked here is confirmation of the text that landed at fusion `16a6387e`, in the header of `hooks/lib/record-client.ts` and in `README-hooks.md` `## Concept`, which says this. A request sent through the client may finish a committed intent, over the intent's full write set, which may reach past the record the caller asked about; it writes only what that writer had already committed and never over a file that diverged. What the Claude side declares is the absence of new mutation requests from a reader, not a write-free read; the declaration grants no reviewer or analyst a record edit of its own and makes no role a read-only sandbox, since no Claude-side agent has a technical role separation. That recovery is admitted for a helper somebody called and for nothing else: no automatic hook reaches the client, by import or by subprocess, which covers the SessionStart, PreToolUse, PostToolUse and SubagentStop commands of `hooks/hooks.json` and every `bin/` helper one of them starts. The route `## Dispatch measurement: option 2` named (the dispatch hook, `bin/fusion-rules`, `bin/fusion-claimed-package`) is cut: the byte measurement left the automatic route at `16a6387e`, new `task_start` rows carry none of the five `bytes_*` fields and no zero or stale value in their place, and the historical rows and cache files are left as they were. `hooks/lib/__tests__/hook-route-exclusion.test.ts` runs every command `hooks/hooks.json` configures against logging stubs, the dispatch once without and once with the old measurement's cache, sees no call to the rule helper, the claimed-package helper or the codec from any automatic route, and pins which compiled modules on those routes import `node:child_process`. A typed refusal and an unanswered call are never read as "no records" or as "nothing claimed". `recovery-blocked` stops the consumer for the paths it names and is reported wherever the protocol reports it, a typed refusal and a finding inside an `ok: true` answer alike (FJ03a steps 3 and 4), and neither helper deletes an intent or repairs divergent bytes. The client never retries: an unanswered call after a mutation is an unknown outcome, repeated only by the caller with the same `operation_id`, and a caller that reads again because `list` and `show` named different revisions makes a new observation, bounded by the caller, and not a retry.

Not asked here: whether `transition` refuses every payload field a kind has no rule about, the question item 25 left open. No reader of FJ03a sends a payload, so it stays open until FJ03c, where the write client is planned and the first Claude-side caller could meet the difference.

FJ03a's hand-over follows as a section of its own at its closing commit, in the shape of `## FJ02b`, and cites what of items 28 to 30 has been answered by then; nothing is assumed.

## FJ03a (the first consumers)

**Written against:** fusion commit `4bbc9d19` on branch `fj-json-workbench` (2026-09-30), the commit of FJ03a step 5 and the last one that moved a file under `codec/`, `bin/` or `hooks/`; it read Prior at commit `ddd4973`, the head of the Prior checkout when this text was written, with no Prior commit after it. `codec/dist/fusion-record.js` at that commit is the FJ02b bundle unmoved, 524 930 bytes, `sha256:5f116c6436175a6b8e1cb08aa2625d2e2f68ef193657b00eb1891ea8d3b965bd`: the same digest on disk, in the blob at `4bbc9d19`, at `6e01977f` and at `7b8dde51`, and the build the codec suite runs reproduces those bytes.

FJ03a puts fusion's own scope and order resolvers on the codec. It is rows 1 to 3 of section 7 and the first of the four parts the FJ03 section above cuts. The commits, in order: `16a6387e` (step 2, the record client and the format gate, and the byte measurement leaves the dispatch hook), `3516d7a2` (3, scope from JSON), `8afcc242` (4, order from JSON), `4bbc9d19` (5, the installed-copy proof and the header of `bin/fusion-record`). The FJ03 section above was committed at `8569da0d` as step 1. Figures re-taken on 2026-09-30 in a scratch clone of `4bbc9d19`, with the git-ignored `hooks/package-lock.json` copied in: `cd codec && CODEC_REQUIRE_GOLDENS=1 npm test` exits 0 with 17 test files and 1 045 tests, `fixtures: 226 manifest entries`, `0 derived-from-source, 13 Go-emitted golden(s)`, `163 of 163 prior_keys exercised`; `npm run typecheck` exits 0; `cd hooks && npm test` exits 0 with 61 test files and 1 023 tests; the scratch tree was clean after both runs. `git diff --stat 4bbc9d19 b6f59e30 -- codec hooks bin` is empty, so the branch head when this text was written carries the same code.

This text is committed after `4bbc9d19`. What changed for Prior's pin: both `Prior: tests/testdata/fusion-fj01/UPSTREAM.json` (104 files) and `Prior: tests/testdata/fusion-codec/UPSTREAM.json` (266 files) pin fusion `7b8dde51`. Hashing each pinned path at `4bbc9d19` finds two that differ, `bin/fusion-record` and this file. In `bin/fusion-record` only the header comment changed (item 6 below), and no executable line did. `git diff --stat 7b8dde51 4bbc9d19 -- codec/src codec/schemas codec/contract codec/dist` names `codec/src/__tests__/install.test.ts` alone, and no file under `agents/`, `skills/`, `rules/`, `templates/`, `docs/` or `.claude-plugin/` changed, nor `install.sh`. No request below asks for a re-pin, since the digest and every replayed byte stand.

### What landed

1. **The record client** (`hooks/lib/record-client.ts`, `16a6387e`). This is the Claude side's counterpart of Prior's `CodecProcess`. `ask` starts the bundle beside the compiled module through `process.execPath`, in the bundle's directory and with an empty environment. It writes one request naming the workbench by absolute path, reads one response, and hands back one of three answers: `result` (with `revisions`), `refused` (class, reason, detail) or `unanswered` (`bundle-missing`, `exit`, `timeout`, `unparseable`). One process runs per request, with no retry, and the timeout is 70 s, above the codec's 65 s lock wait, so a typed `conflict/lock-timeout` arrives first. A caller never reads `refused` or `unanswered` as an empty store.
2. **The format gate** (`gate` in the same module). It sends `inspect` before any other request. On `json-control` it hands back the workbench id. Otherwise it hands back the state by name (`legacy`, or `unsupported` with the codec's diagnosis), `refused` or `unanswered`. This is the rule item 28 described as the one fusion applies meanwhile, and every FJ03a consumer asks it first. The Claude side sends four operations in all: `inspect`, `list`, `show` and `reconcile`.
3. **Scope: `bin/fusion-claimed-package`** (`hooks/lib/scope.ts` through the entry `hooks/scope.ts`, `3516d7a2`). A package is this checkout's when its `status` is `claimed` and its `claim.checkout_id` equals the identifier `bin/fusion-identity` prints. The sequence runs the gate, then `list`, then `show` of each claimed row at the revision `list` named. When a revision moved between the two, the read starts over from `list` once: that is a new observation and not a retry, and a second difference is `store-changing`. Output and exit table are unchanged. Exit 0 prints `PACKAGE=` and `CONTAINER=`, or nothing when no package is held. Exit 1 means no workbench, or `bin/fusion-identity` stopped. Exit 2 is a usage error. Exit 3 means the item in scope cannot be determined, with nothing on stdout and no fall back to the shared store. Exit 3 gains its causes by name: two or more claims naming this checkout (each named), `legacy`, `unsupported`, a read the codec refused (`recovery-blocked` among them, as a typed refusal or as a `problem` row inside an `ok: true` list), a read it did not answer, a package row that does not read, and `store-changing`. `bin/fusion-stores` and the v11 container root are no longer consulted. `bin/fusion-paths <agent> <item-dir>` asks the same entry whether the directory names a package: exit 1 when it names none, exit 3 when the workbench is refused.
4. **Order: `bin/fusion-work-order`** (`hooks/lib/work-graph.ts` through `hooks/order.ts`, `8afcc242`). The reader gates, sends `list`, then sends `reconcile`. It reads `reconcile`'s `records` findings before its `dependencies`, and places each dependency entry by the table in the module header. The graph algorithm (Tarjan, Kahn, depth, blocks, the paused override) is the pure `orderOf` and is unchanged. The hook side evaluates no dependency condition; `reconcile`'s `dependencySatisfied` is the one implementation. A dependency on a terminal package under `terminal` is satisfied and prints no row. A dependency on a dropped package under `succeeded` blocks the dependent and prints an `unmet=` row. The exit codes are 0 (the check ran; a cycle and `verdict=empty` are answers), 1 (usage), 2 (no workbench), 3 (the compiled hooks or the bundle missing) and 4, which is new. Exit 4 means the workbench was not read: the state or refusal goes on stderr and nothing goes on stdout. The `note=` line says, as Prior's review asked under `## Order output and activation`, that a satisfied terminal target is now told apart from an unresolved one, that an unresolved prerequisite is no proof that the condition holds, and that the report authorises no dispatch.
5. **What the executors found beyond the plan**, each stated in the module headers:
   - (a) `list` is sent without a scope, by both readers. On a JSON-controlled workbench whose container store does not exist yet, `list` with `scope: "work-packages"` is refused `unknown-scope/scope-missing`, and reading that refusal as an empty store is the one thing a scope resolver must not do. Without a scope the same workbench answers `ok: true` with `records: []`, and the readers take the rows whose control file is `package.json` under the container store. Re-measured through the bundle over a directory holding only the fixture's `workbench.json` and `.fusion-setup`. The FJ03a plan's "`list` over `work-packages`" is inexact by this detail.
   - (b) A `reconcile` finding against a live package other than `recovery-blocked` moves that package out of the graph. Examples are `schema-invalid`, a state-rule finding, `narrative-missing` and `foreign-workbench-id`. The package is counted in `unreadable-head=` and named on an `unreadable=` row. An edge naming it prints `unresolved=` with reason `target-unreadable`, because the codec withholds the edges of a record it reports against and the reader cannot tell from outside which finding withheld which edge. For `narrative-missing` this is stricter than it needs to be, and the header says so.
   - (c) `unmet-edges=` is a new count key, the number of `unmet=` rows.
   - (d) A `dependency-unmet` entry whose target this read did not list prints `unresolved=` with reason `target-unlisted`. The reader does not read again.
   - (e) Exit 4 reaches further than the gate. It covers every read refused or unanswered after the gate as well: a `list` or `reconcile` refusal, a missing answer, and `recovery-blocked` wherever the protocol reports it.
6. **The installed copy, and the wrapper's header** (`4bbc9d19`). Two cases in `codec/src/__tests__/install.test.ts` install a `git archive` of the tree through `install.sh` into a scratch home and run the shipped helpers over a JSON-controlled workbench inside a `git init` project. Every package in it is written by the kernel through the installed `bin/fusion-record`, and `node` is the only runtime. First, `bin/fusion-claimed-package` answers the empty store with exit 0 and nothing printed. After two claims, one of them for another checkout, it prints exactly this checkout's `PACKAGE=` and `CONTAINER=`. It refuses a legacy workbench with exit 3, empty stdout and `is legacy` on stderr. Second, `bin/fusion-work-order` prints the order over two packages and one `terminal` edge, and refuses a legacy workbench with exit 4 and empty stdout. The header of `bin/fusion-record` and its roster row in `README-hooks.md` now name the thirteen operations the bundle answers and `migration` as deferred, and point at `inspect`. Both had said that `migration` is refused `not-implemented-in-fj01`. The bundle answers `{"class":"operation-unknown","reason":"not-implemented"}`, re-sent through the bundle as `{"op":"migration","phase":"survey"}`, which request 24 above already stated correctly.
7. **The cost of one scope resolution.** The fixture is a `git init` project whose workbench holds the fixture manifest and one package, created and claimed through the bundle for this checkout. Ten calls of `bin/fusion-claimed-package` from the scratch clone took 6.67 s, 0.67 s each, printing the two lines with exit 0. Ten calls of `bin/fusion-paths analyst` took 7.14 s, 0.71 s each. Ten bare `inspect` spawns of the bundle took 1.84 s, 0.18 s each. All three ran on Node 25.7.0 on the fusion author's machine. One resolution is the gate, `list` and one `show`, three spawns, plus `bin/fusion-identity` and the shell around them. That is under the one second the FJ03a plan set as its stopping clause, so no additive `list` detail is asked for. The figure is what scope resolution adds to an agent's Setup, and as much again where a project's context manifest makes `bin/fusion-rules` resolve a topic.
8. **This repository's own workbench is refused, as planned.** It has no `workbench.json`, so from `3516d7a2` on the work tree's helpers answer it by name. Run from the scratch clone of `4bbc9d19`, `bin/fusion-claimed-package` exits 3 naming `legacy`, and `bin/fusion-work-order` exits 4 with 0 bytes on stdout. Sessions here run the installed v12 release and are unaffected. Installing a build of this branch waits for the migration of this workbench, which is FJ04's under the order item 26 records.

### Recovery and rollout consequences

The FJ02b section above says FJ03's consumers on the Claude side must honour the three statements of `Prior: docs/design/fusion-fj02-prior-response.md` `## Recovery and rollout consequences`. FJ03a honours each as follows.

- **A read may finish a committed intent.** The declaration stands in the header of `hooks/lib/record-client.ts` and in `README-hooks.md` `## Concept`, as item 30 quotes it. What the Claude side declares is the absence of new mutation requests from a reader, not a write-free read. The recovery is admitted for a helper somebody called and for nothing else. No automatic hook reaches the client, by import or by subprocess, and `hooks/lib/__tests__/hook-route-exclusion.test.ts` pins that on every command `hooks/hooks.json` configures. Neither resolver deletes an intent or repairs divergent bytes. `recovery-blocked` stops both with a named failure.
- **One compatible codec pin per workbench.** The client resolves the bundle relative to its own compiled module, so an install runs the bundle it shipped with and a work tree runs its own. There is no upward walk and no environment variable that could hand a request to another tree's bundle. One install therefore carries one bundle. A workbench is served by one pin as long as the installations writing to it agree on the release, which the activation window of item 26 governs.
- **No FJ01 writer beside a later one.** The Claude side has no FJ01 writer. No Claude-side code sent a mutation before FJ03a, and FJ03a sends none: the four operations of item 2 are reads. The only Claude-side writers of the codec are the test helpers, which run against temporary workbenches, and the recorded sessions under `codec/fixtures/`.

**Claim ownership under response 22.** FJ03a sends no `claim`, `release` or `transition`. The ownership check that Prior's option (a) places on the host is therefore not exercised on the Claude side yet: deciding ownership from `show` and sending that record's revision as `expected_revision`. FJ03c's write client is the first Claude-side caller that exercises it. Scope resolution reads a claim as the assignment it is, and whether its holder is authorised stays the host's question.

### 31. Whether Prior's 1 MiB response bound applies to codec answers, and how `list` is served on a large workbench

**Closes:** fusion decision `260929-2025_*_which-response-size-does-the-claude-side-client-accept-from-the-codec-and-which-environment-does-the-child-get.md`, option 3, narrowed by the user on 2026-09-30: fusion changes no code, keeps its bound as a safety net only, and asks Prior this one question. Preferred form: a reply that says whether the 1 MiB bound applies to codec answers and, if it does, names one of the three ways below.

The two hosts, as they stand. The Claude side's `ask` in `hooks/lib/record-client.ts` (fusion `ccf5ef6a`) starts the child with `env: {}` and `maxBuffer: MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES`, which is 16 MiB (`16 * 1024 * 1024`). A larger answer is `unanswered` and never truncated into a result. Scope resolution then exits 3, so an agent's Setup halts, and `bin/fusion-work-order` exits 4. The 16 MiB guards against a defective child and is no contract figure, and fusion does not choose a contract bound alone. A hung child is already guarded by the 70 s timeout (item 1 above). Prior's `CodecProcess.Call` in `Prior: internal/fusionhost/codec_process.go` at Prior `ddd4973` sets `cmd.Env = []string{}` and bounds its output through `strictjson.MaxBytes`, which `Prior: internal/strictjson/reader.go` defines as `1 << 20`. A larger answer is `ErrCodecOutcomeUnknown` there, and neither file changed in `ddd4973..ad21e58`. Both hosts already give the child an empty environment, so nothing is asked about it: the codec reads only `FUSION_WORKBENCH` from its environment, as a default (`codec/src/cli/main.ts`), and the Claude client names the absolute workbench in every request.

The measurement, taken on 2026-09-30 through the bundle above, run with an empty environment over scratch copies of `codec/fixtures/workbench/`. Both FJ03a readers send `list` without a scope (item 5(a) above), so the answer carries every control file the codec walks under the workbench root. An unscoped `list` over the fixture's three records answers 1 380 bytes. After `create` of 1 000 packages through the bundle, their directory names taken from this repository's 46 containers and of the same lengths (44.8 characters on average), the answer is 461 504 bytes for 1 003 records, and it grows by 460 bytes per package row, linearly at 49, 203, 503 and 1 003 records. A record in a deeper store, a plan beside its package, say, carries a longer path, so 460 is the low end per row. At 460 bytes a row, about 2 270 records fit in 1 MiB and about 36 000 in 16 MiB.

This repository's own workbench is the first one that will meet the bound. It holds 2 442 Markdown files under `fusion-workbench/` outside `archive/`, and 3 049 with `archive/` included. What it will list after migration is an extrapolation, not a measurement. Not every Markdown file becomes a record: reports and analyses without machine-read state stay Markdown (section 2.2 of the specification), and a terminal record outside the archive receives at most extracted metadata (section 2.1). But if every file outside `archive/` became a record at 460 bytes a row, the answer would be about 1 123 000 bytes, over the 1 048 576 of 1 MiB. Whether `archive/` enters `list` after migration is only half established. The codec's walk (`controlFiles` in `codec/src/store.ts`) descends into every directory except dot entries, so an unscoped `list` lists whatever control files `archive/` holds. The specification keeps `archive/` as historical storage with no mass rewrite, and archives a pair's two files together (section 3, the layout tree and the paragraph under it, at Prior `ad21e58`). So the existing archive would not enter `list` at migration, but every pair archived after it would.

The question: does Prior's 1 MiB bound apply to answers from the codec? If it does, how is `list` on a large workbench to be served: a higher bound for the codec channel, paging in `list`, or a scope the readers can use? A scope is not a way out as the readers stand: on a JSON-controlled workbench whose container store does not exist yet, `list` with `scope: "work-packages"` is refused `unknown-scope/scope-missing` (item 5(a) above). The answer is needed before FJ04's real migrations, and it does not block FJ03b.

## FJ03b (questions before the observers)

**Written against:** fusion commit `b1dcd3c6` on branch `fj-json-workbench` (2026-09-30), the head of the branch when this text was written. The FJ03b plan (`260930-1451_*_plan-fj03b-observers-checkers-citations-and-the-monitor-on-json.md`) names `c6c25d3e`; the review follow-up has landed since, at `b1dcd3c6`, and moved files under `hooks/` and `bin/` but none under `codec/`. It read Prior at commit `ad21e58`, the head of the Prior checkout, with no Prior commit after it on any ref; the specification is quoted from that commit's object. `codec/dist/fusion-record.js` is the FJ02b bundle unmoved, 524 930 bytes, `sha256:5f116c6436175a6b8e1cb08aa2625d2e2f68ef193657b00eb1891ea8d3b965bd`, on disk and in the blob at `b1dcd3c6`.

FJ03b is the second of the four parts the FJ03 section above cuts. It carries three rows of section 7 in code: row 7 ("Hooks/root/status-/citation-/staging-Verbraucher"), row 8 ("Monitor und Events") and row 10 ("Stores/Path-Lints/Tracking"). It also carries the check and sweep half of row 9 ("Zitierprüfung, Sweep, Archivierung"). Its plan splits every consumer by route, and `hooks/hooks.json` fixes the route of each.

- **The automatic route** is the six compiled entries `hooks/hooks.json` runs (`session-start`, `session-id`, `identity-notice`, `guard`, `tracker`, `subagent-stop`) and the shell lines beside them. It learns the JSON surfaces as names and reads no record. Staging drift classifies `workbench.json`, `.json-state/` and the three control-file shapes (`package.json`, `*.record.json`, `*.evidence.json`) by path. It gains one fault, `pair-split`, for a pair of which exactly one half is staged. The citation index leaves out control files and `.json-state/`, so a pair is one citable artefact. The names are held in one place and pinned equal to the codec's constants in `codec/src/store.ts`. No entry on this route opens a control file or reaches the codec, so the recovery declaration confirmed under item 30 stands as worded.
- **The explicit route** is `bin/fusion-citation-check`, `bin/fusion-citation-sweep` and `bin/fusion-plan-size`, which a person or an agent calls. All three read through one new module, `hooks/lib/record-index.ts`. It asks the gate and sends `list` without a scope. It takes each row's validity from the one criterion the review follow-up put in `hooks/lib/codec-read.ts`. It takes liveness from the `terminal` set of `codec/contract/transitions.json`, the table the kernel reads. The index is built per call and persisted nowhere. On a JSON-controlled workbench an old marker basename, a new marker-free name and a UUID reference resolve to the same record, and two files matching one citation are a conflict (section 4.4). Before `--yes`, the sweep names every rewrite that would make a bound plan adoption or evidence record stale, and it refuses nothing on that ground.
- **The monitor** reads event rows and nothing else. Item 32 is about its row.

**What FJ03b leaves as it is.** None of these files changed between `c6c25d3e` and `b1dcd3c6`. `hooks/lib/review-coverage.ts` reads `**Reviewed-range:**` of review reports, which stay Markdown (section 2.2). `hooks/lib/edge-answers.ts` reads curator-run reports. `hooks/lib/workbench-root.ts` walks up to `.fusion-setup`, which a JSON workbench keeps (section 3, class R3). `hooks/session-start.ts` counts sources outside the workbench. The lints that read this repository's own workbench are FJ03d's under item 26, and so is the rule text with the layout tree and the tracking classes. The archive half of row 9 is FJ03c's.

**Why the explicit checkers keep a legacy branch where FJ03a's resolvers refuse.** A scope resolver that read a legacy workbench as empty would answer "nothing claimed" and fall silently to the shared store, which row 1 forbids. So FJ03a refuses legacy by name, with exit 3 or exit 4. The checkers of FJ03b answer questions that have a correct legacy answer today, and section 9's FJ03b row asks for exactly that: "aktive Legacy-Sitzungen behalten passende Regeln und Leser". The order paragraph under that table adds that, during FJ03b and FJ03c, the rules and live predicates which active legacy sessions read from the work tree stay format-compatible. So each checker gates, with three outcomes:

- `json-control`: it reads the JSON.
- `legacy`: it runs the entry's existing reader unchanged and prints a `format=legacy` line first.
- `unsupported`, a typed refusal (`recovery-blocked` among them) or no answer: it refuses by name, with nothing on stdout. The exit code is new: 4 for the check and the plan-size check, 6 for the sweep, whose codes 4 and 5 are its two guards.

FJ03d removes each legacy branch or narrows it to archive read mode, in the window item 26 records. The evidence that the legacy readers are kept is a diff. The four helpers, staging drift included, run over this repository's own legacy workbench before and after FJ03b, and the only permitted difference is the added `format=legacy` line.

**FJ03b does not depend on item 31.** At `b1dcd3c6` two readers send an unscoped `list`, `hooks/lib/scope.ts` and `hooks/lib/work-graph.ts`. FJ03b adds three consumers of it, the three checkers above, and all three go through `hooks/lib/record-index.ts`. An answer to item 31 that brings paging or a usable scope therefore changes one module on FJ03b's side. FJ03b is not held for it.

### 32. The `record_change` row the monitor reads, and whether Prior writes it

**Closes:** fusion decision `260930-1451_*_where-does-the-monitor-take-a-records-status-from-and-how-does-an-event-name-a-record-and-its-host.md`, answered by the user on 2026-09-30 (option 1, with the corrections folded in before approval); the shape FJ03c's write client writes; and, on the Prior side, the host half of row 8 of section 7 ("neue Ereignisse mit Host-/Record-Bezug"). Preferred form: a reply to (a) to (c) below. The row is stated for information. Fusion answered the record and FJ03b's readers do not wait for the reply. FJ03c's writer does, since FJ03c is already gated on this item.

**Why the monitor takes its status from events.** Row 8 asks for "Status aus JSON". The monitor cannot ask the codec as the helpers do. `bin/monitor` is a bash-wrapped Python server that `/fusion:setup` copies verbatim into the workbench and the user starts by hand. It polls every 2 s (`INTERVAL=2`) and has no plugin root at poll time. A monitor that ran the codec would send reads that may finish a committed intent on its own schedule, which the declaration of item 30 does not admit. The hooks cannot supply the status either, since no automatic hook reads a control file or reaches the codec. So the panel answers a narrower question: the last observed change of the running work item, by which host, at which revision. An explicit mutation route writes it after the codec has answered. The panel says "observed", not "current". A hand edit, a change made before the row kind existed, or a write by a host that logs nothing stays invisible until the next logged change. The current state stays the answer of `bin/fusion-work-order`, which a person calls.

**The row.** One JSON object on one line, appended to `fusion-workbench/orchestrator-events.jsonl`. **There is one row per record written, not one per operation.**

| Field | Value | Taken from |
|---|---|---|
| `ts` | the fixed-width UTC stamp, below | the writer's clock |
| `event` | `"record_change"` | fixed |
| `host` | `"claude"`, or `"prior"` if Prior writes rows | the writer |
| `op` | the request's `op` | the request |
| `operation_id` | the request's `operation_id`; the rows of one operation share it | the request |
| `workbench_id` | the manifest's id | the gate's `inspect` |
| `record_id` | the record's id | the writer's `show` before the mutation, which the ownership check of response 22 requires anyway; for `create`, the id in the request, fixed in advance |
| `path` | the workbench-relative control path, as the protocol's `record_selector` defines it | the request, or the answer |
| `kind` | the record's kind (`package`, `issue`, `plan`, `discussion`, `decision`, `evidence`), never the operation's | that `show`; for `create`, the request |
| `revision` | this path's entry in the answer's `revisions` map | the answer |
| `change` | per operation, below | the answer and the request |
| `person`, `checkout`, `session_id` | who wrote it | the host's identity; see (b) |

No answer carries a `record_id`. Requests name a record by `path`. The results of `transition`, `set-mode`, `set-dependencies` and `adopt-plan` carry `path` and `revision` (`codec/src/cli/ops.ts`), and `claim` and `release` answer as the transition they are. That is why the id comes from the writer's `show`. Nothing is read from disk to write a row.

`change`, per operation:

| Operation | `change` |
|---|---|
| `transition`, `claim`, `release` | `{from, to}` |
| `create` | `{created: <status>}` |
| `set-mode` | `{mode}` |
| `set-dependencies` | `{depends_on: <count>}` |
| `adopt-plan` | `{adopted: <role>}` on the package row, `{adopted_as: <role>}` on the document row, `{replaced_by: <record_id>}` on the replaced plan's row |

A row that moves no status carries no `from` or `to`; it does not repeat the old status. `adopt-plan` writes the package, the document and, where it replaces a live plan, that plan, in one intent, so it gives two or three rows sharing one `operation_id`. Each row's `revision` is that path's entry in `revisions`. `attach-evidence` is a mutation the bundle answers today (it writes the package alone) and the ruling defines no `change` for it. Fusion sets that `change` in FJ03c's plan, before any row is written.

**What the monitor does with it.** The panel's work item is a container basename that the dispatch hook copies from the prompt (`task_start.work_item`). A row matches exactly when its `path` is `work-packages/<work_item>/package.json`. The panel shows the newest matching row that carries `change.to` or `change.created`, as "last observed: `<status>` at `<revision, 12 hex>` by `<host>`, `<hh:mm>`", or "no observed change" when none matches. Rows that move no status appear on the event log page and never as status. The monitor keeps only this checkout's rows for everything else (`_read_events`). It reads `record_change` rows in a pass of their own before that filter, so a newer row from another checkout or with `host: "prior"` wins. Old rows, the retired kinds and the gate strings render as before, and nothing rewrites the log.

**The timestamp, and how a line reaches the log.** The monitor orders rows by the raw `ts` string, with a stable sort, so rows sharing a `ts` keep file order. A revision hash gives no order and is never used for one. Ordering by the raw string is correct only while every writer writes the same form. That form is the log's: `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS`, UTC, 19 characters, no fraction and no designator (`utcStamp` in `hooks/lib/orchestrator-events.ts`). All 5 157 rows of the log at `b1dcd3c6` carry it. It is not the `Z`-suffixed millisecond form of `Date.toISOString()`, which the guard's own log under `.guard-state/` uses. A Prior writer has to use exactly this form. The log is class R2 (`rules/workbench-tracking.md`): tracked, appended by every checkout, never rewritten, and merged by git with `merge=union`. A writer appends whole newline-terminated lines and never rewrites or reorders the file.

Three questions:

- **(a)** Will Prior's host append `record_change` rows to the same log, with `host: "prior"`, for the mutations it sends through the codec? If it will not, the monitor shows only Claude-side changes. A Prior-written transition then stays invisible until the next Claude-side row for that record, which is the limitation the ruling accepts.
- **(b)** Which fields does Prior need beyond the table, and which can it not fill? In particular: on the Claude side `person` and `checkout` come from `bin/fusion-identity`, and `session_id` from the hook payload. The Prior host has neither a `.checkout-id` nor a Claude session. Will it fill the three with values of its own, write them `null`, or leave them out? The panel reads only `ts`, `event`, `host`, `path`, `kind`, `revision` and `change`, and joins on `path`.
- **(c)** Please confirm the `ts` form above for Prior's rows. And if Prior binds evidence through `attach-evidence`, say whether it would write a row for it, so that FJ03c's `change` for that operation serves both hosts.

### What landed

1. **The JSON surface names, on the automatic route** (`hooks/lib/stores.ts`, `ba6105d9`). One module names `WORKBENCH_MANIFEST`, `JSON_STATE_DIR`, `PACKAGE_CONTROL`, `RECORD_CONTROL_SUFFIX`, `EVIDENCE_SUFFIX`, `isControlFile` and `narrativeOf`, and `fusion-stores.test.ts` holds each equal to its counterpart in `codec/src/store.ts` by reading that file's text. `narrativeOf` maps `<stem>.record.json` to `<stem>.md`, `package.json` to the container's own `<dir>.md`, and `<stem>[.<n>].evidence.json` to the report `<stem>.md`. Nothing on this route opens a control file: every answer is decided from the path, `git status` and a directory listing.
2. **Staging drift** (`hooks/lib/staging-drift.ts`, `ba6105d9`). `workbench.json` classifies `in-flight` as class R3 and `.json-state/` `in-flight` as class L, in a list of their own, `JSON_LIVE_STATE`, until the tracking rule names them (FJ03d). A control file classifies `record` wherever its narrative would. A pair of which exactly one half is staged gets the fault code `pair-split` on the unstaged half. That row stays in class `record` and prints `PAIR-SPLIT` in place of `UNSTAGED`, naming the staged half. The class stays `record` because `agents/orchestrator.md` `## Staging check` names four classes, says what to do per class, and FJ03b leaves that file alone; "stage it" is the right act for the row. Exit codes unchanged.
3. **The citation index** (`hooks/lib/citation-scan.ts`, `bf3aea49`). The basename index leaves out every control file and everything under `.json-state/`, so a pair is one citable artefact and a `.md`-less prefix citation of a marker-free record has one hit. The tracker's citation-form measurement uses the same index, with its output unchanged in shape.
4. **The record index, on the explicit route** (`hooks/lib/record-index.ts`, new at `bf3aea49`). `readRecordIndex` asks the gate, then sends `list` and `reconcile` without a scope, as FJ03a's readers do (item 5(a) of the FJ03a section). It takes each row's validity from the one criterion in `hooks/lib/codec-read.ts`. It answers `json-control` with `byNarrative`, `byId`, `byControl`, `unreadable` and `unresolvedRefs`, the last from `reconcile`'s `references` with reason `record-not-found`. The other answers are `legacy`, or `unknown` with its cause: `unsupported`, `refused` (`recovery-blocked` among the refusals, as a typed refusal or as a finding inside an `ok: true` answer) or `unanswered`. `live` is a status outside the kind's `terminal` set in `codec/contract/transitions.json`, the table the kernel reads. The index is built per call and persisted nowhere, so a pull is seen on the next call. On a JSON-controlled workbench the Claude side still sends four operations and no more: `inspect`, `list`, `show` and `reconcile`.
5. **`bin/fusion-citation-check`** (`hooks/citation-check.ts`, `bf3aea49`). The first line is `format=`. On `json-control`, `verdict=` is scoped by `live` from the index, and a narrative without a record counts as not live. An `ambiguous` hit is a `conflict` violation (section 4.4). Two keys are new, `conflict=` and `uuid-unresolved=`, the second with one row per unresolved reference; `uuid-unresolved=` does not move `verdict=`. Exit codes: 0 (ran), 1 (usage), 2 (no workbench), 3 (compiled hooks or bundle missing, or an internal error) and 4, which is new. Exit 4 means the workbench was not read: the cause goes on stderr and nothing goes on stdout. One difference from legacy that the plan did not name: an open discussion counts as live on JSON, where the legacy reader scopes it out. The step's tests resolve an old concrete-marker citation, its wildcard form, a new marker-free name and a `depends_on` UUID each to its record by id. They need two records to do so, because the kernel admits no single record citable in all four forms at once.
6. **`bin/fusion-citation-sweep`** (`hooks/citation-sweep.ts`, `7c8a7dd1`). It gates through the record index before it reads the corpus, and prints `format=` first. On `json-control` a `<path>` argument naming a file only the codec writes is a usage error, exit 1: a control file, the manifest, or anything under `.json-state/`, since section 1.5 covers all codec JSON. A declared `citations.extraPaths` entry of that kind is left out with a stderr line. Before the summary, the census prints `bound=<file>  <role>:<control>` for each file in the write set that a package's `active_documents` entry or an evidence record's `report` binds by hash. So guard (b) names, before `--yes`, every rewrite that would make a plan adoption or an evidence record stale. The sweep refuses nothing on that ground, since a rewrite is revertible under guard (a) and the staleness is the codec's to report. Exit 6 is new and means the workbench was not read, with nothing on stdout; exit 3 covers a missing bundle, as in the check. Exits 4 and 5 stay guards (a) and (b). Two gaps are stated in the entry's header. A binding whose target is not in the index names no file. An evidence record's `brief_revision` and `plan_revision` bind by hash alone and name no path, so no `bound=` line comes from them.
7. **`bin/fusion-plan-size`** (`hooks/plan-size.ts`, `hooks/lib/plan-size.ts`, `c2d1c7f3`). The entry gates before anything is read and prints `format=` first. On `json-control` it measures every plan record whose `live` is true, at its narrative. On `legacy` the filename markers (`LIVE_MARKERS`) decide as before. Both formats go through one shared weighing. A plan control file that `list` or `reconcile` judged unreadable is counted on a JSON-only `unreadable=` line and named in a row. Its liveness is exactly what did not read, and dropping it would give a silent `verdict=empty`. The spec exclusion also strips the marker-free `YYMMDD-HHMM-` stamp, so it works on a JSON narrative's name. Exit 4 is new, with the check's meaning, and exit 3 matches the check's.
8. **The monitor** (`bin/monitor`, `c74c41ac`), as the decision item 32 closes rules it. `record_change` rows are read in a pass of their own over the whole log, outside the own-checkout filter, and sorted by the raw `ts` string with a stable sort. The session, mode and dispatch readings leave every `record_change` row out, this checkout's own included, so they see what they saw before the kind existed. For a running dispatch that named a work item, the dashboard shows a `Last observed:` line under `Work item:`. It is built from the newest row whose `path` is `work-packages/<item>/package.json` and whose `change` carries `to` or `created`: status, the revision's first 12 hex, host, and UTC `hh:mm`. Where no row matches it says `no observed change`. `/api/dashboard` serves the rows as `record_changes`, in their own window of `MAX_EVENTS`, so a burst from another checkout cannot push this session's rows out. The monitor reads no record, runs no codec and gains no argument. `hooks/lib/events-query.ts` is unchanged: a `record_change` row changes no presence answer and is not counted as malformed. Nothing writes a `record_change` row yet; the writer is FJ03c's write client, at the shape item 32 confirms.
9. **The installed copy** (`codec/src/__tests__/install.test.ts`, `08987ce1`). A third installed-copy case, in the shape of FJ03a's two. The project is a `git init` directory whose two packages, one `set-dependencies` edge and one adopted plan the installed `bin/fusion-record` wrote. The four helpers run from the install with `node` the only runtime beside the tools `bin/fusion-identity` needs. `bin/fusion-citation-check` prints `format=json-control` and resolves the plan by its name and by its `.md`-less prefix, with `conflict=0`. `bin/fusion-plan-size` prints `plans=1` and `unreadable=0`. `bin/fusion-citation-sweep --dry-run` prints one `bound=` line, the adopted plan against its package. After the brief is edited and staged and a `claim` rewrites `package.json` unstaged, `bin/fusion-staging-drift` prints one `PAIR-SPLIT` row on `package.json` naming the staged brief. The case ran and passed in the scratch clone above.

The runs were `fusion-citation-check`, the same with `--undecidable`, `fusion-plan-size`, `fusion-citation-sweep --dry-run`, `fusion-citation-sweep --repair --dry-run` and `fusion-staging-drift`. ### Recovery and rollout consequences

The three statements of `Prior: docs/design/fusion-fj02-prior-response.md` `## Recovery and rollout consequences` hold for FJ03b as FJ03a states them above. FJ03b changes nothing in that account, for these reasons.

- **A read may finish a committed intent.** The three checkers that now reach the codec are explicit helpers, which somebody calls; the declaration item 30 confirmed admits recovery for exactly that route. No automatic entry reaches them. `hooks/hooks.json` and `hooks/lib/__tests__/hook-route-exclusion.test.ts` are unchanged between `c6c25d3e` and `08987ce1`, so the pinned list of spawning modules and the import assertion stand as they were, and the test is green. Following the relative imports of the six compiled entries `hooks/hooks.json` runs (`guard`, `identity-notice`, `session-id`, `session-start`, `subagent-stop`, `tracker`) at `08987ce1` reaches none of `record-index`, `record-client`, `codec-read` or `plan-size`. The monitor reaches no codec either. `recovery-blocked` stops each checker with a named failure, and no checker deletes an intent or repairs divergent bytes.
- **One compatible codec pin per workbench.** Unchanged: the record index asks through the FJ03a client, which resolves the bundle beside its own compiled module.
- **No FJ01 writer beside a later one.** FJ03b sends no mutation. The sweep rewrites Markdown only and refuses a codec file as its target. The first Claude-side writer of the codec remains FJ03c's write client.

### No request for a binding field in `list`

The FJ03b plan measured one cost, to decide whether to ask for one. The sweep's `bound=` pass sends one `show` per package and evidence row, since `list` carries neither `active_documents` nor an evidence record's `report`. The plan's criterion was that a figure a user would not wait for becomes a request for a binding field in `list`. Step 4 measured a dry run over 40 packages with one adopted plan each at 8.4 to 8.7 s over three runs, on the fusion author's machine. Of that, the 40 `show` calls took 7.3 s, about 180 ms per spawn, and `inspect`, `list` and `reconcile` about 190 ms each. Re-taken for this text: twenty `show` spawns of the bundle, with an empty environment, over a scratch copy of `codec/fixtures/workbench/` took 3.65 s, 182 ms each, on Node 25.7.0.

Fusion asks nothing, because both bindings can be read from answers the protocol already gives. First, an evidence record's report is its neighbour by the naming rule the kernel enforces (`unknown-scope/report-not-neighbour`, item 8 of the FJ02 section), so the report path follows from the control path, which `narrativeOf` already maps. Second, a package's active documents are reference sites in `reconcile`'s `references`, which the record index already requests. The recorded `codec/fixtures/protocol-session-fj02/15-reconcile.response.json` carries such an entry: `path` the package, `at` `/active_documents/0/ref`, `status` `resolved`, and `target` the plan's control path. Only the entry's role is not in that answer. So the per-row `show` is a fusion-side cost that fusion can remove. Adding a field to `list` for data `reconcile` already carries would move the digest both hosts pinned, for no new information. If fusion later needs the role in `list` as well, it will be asked for with the `initialize` revision (item 27), which moves the digest anyway, as item 28 proposed for `state`.

FJ03c is planned against `08987ce1` and the bundle digest above. Its preconditions stay those the FJ03b plan names: this plan closed, the `initialize` plan (item 27) closed and qualified by the Prior side, and item 32 answered.

## One qualified revision (the contract delta before the kernel change)

**Written against:** fusion commit `3c0903f4` on branch `fj-json-workbench` (2026-09-30), the head of the branch when this text was written and the commit that approved the plan it serves (`260930-1654_*_plan-initialize-the-codec-creates-a-new-workbench-and-list-names-its-state.md`). The plan was drafted against `b3909330`, and `git diff --stat b3909330 3c0903f4 -- codec bin hooks` is empty, so the code this delta starts from is the code at `b3909330`. It read Prior at commit `a15dfc8`, the head of the Prior checkout; `git log --all a15dfc8..` there is empty, so no Prior commit follows it on any ref. The specification is quoted from that commit's object. `codec/dist/fusion-record.js` is the FJ02b bundle unmoved, 524 930 bytes, `sha256:5f116c6436175a6b8e1cb08aa2625d2e2f68ef193657b00eb1891ea8d3b965bd`: the same digest on disk, in the blob at `3c0903f4` and in the blob at `b3909330`. Both `Prior: tests/testdata/fusion-fj01/UPSTREAM.json` (104 files) and `Prior: tests/testdata/fusion-codec/UPSTREAM.json` (266 files) at `a15dfc8` still pin fusion `7b8dde51` at that digest, and `git diff --stat 7b8dde51 3c0903f4 -- codec/src codec/schemas codec/contract codec/dist` names `codec/src/__tests__/install.test.ts` alone.

This section is the contract delta that Prior asked for in `Prior: docs/design/fusion-initialize-reconcile-plan-amendment.md` at `a15dfc8`. It is written before any kernel change. That review chose option 1: the `reconcile` performance fix and the plan/spec binding role ride the same qualified codec revision as `initialize` (item 27) and `list.result.state` (item 28, closure (a)). The specification records the same in section 4.1, "Ergänzung vom 30. September". On that basis fusion folds four changes into one revision, qualified by one re-pin at the hand-over. As `a15dfc8` asks, only the changed sections of the contract follow. Everything not named here stays as the FJ02, FJ02b and FJ03a sections above state it. Nothing below is frozen: the digest is fixed only at the hand-over, and a correction Prior asks for before then lands in this revision.

`initialize` depends on neither item 31 nor item 32. Item 31 stays relevant to the answer size of `reconcile` and `list`, and a fast answer can still exceed 1 MiB, as `a15dfc8` says. The measurements under "The measurement protocol" below report output bytes for that item. They do not answer it. One new item, 33, states a fusion decision for Prior to object to.

### `initialize`

**The request.** `{op: "initialize", workbench, operation_id, id}`. `workbench` is required on this branch. `id` is the caller's workbench UUID. A manifest field sent by the caller is `schema-invalid/request`: the codec writes the manifest, and no host serialises one. `initialize` is added to the protocol schema's `op` enum after `validate`, with a `oneOf` branch of its own and `additionalProperties: false`.

**The manifest written.** `fusion.workbench/v1` with the caller's `id`, `required_features: ["json-control-v1"]`, `migration: null` and `extensions: {}`, serialised by the codec's serialiser and validated before the intent is written. `workbench.schema.json` already admits it.

**The answer.** `{operation_id, id, path: "workbench.json", revision}`, with `revisions` beside the result as on every mutation. The answer carries no absolute path.

**The order.** The kernel runs its one mutation sequence: replay, intent, recovery and lock stay as the FJ02 section above states them. The state gate becomes a parameter. Every other mutation still admits only `json-control`, and `initialize` admits every state. The steps, in order, each ending the request where it answers:

1. The request is validated under `fusion.protocol/v1`. An invalid one is `schema-invalid/request`.
2. A target that is absent or is not a directory is `unknown-scope/workbench-missing`.
3. If `.json-state` is absent or is not a directory, the content check runs once without the lock, since no intent, stored answer or lock can exist there. A `workbench.json` entry of any kind is `conflict/manifest-present`. Any other entry is `conflict/target-not-empty`, and a `.json-state` that is not a directory is such an entry. Nothing is written on either refusal. An empty directory goes on to step 4.
4. The lock is taken (a wait beyond 65 s is `conflict/lock-timeout`), the sweep runs, and every pending intent is recovered, as for any mutation.
5. If this request's `operation_id` names a pending intent that recovery left blocked, the same request is `operation-unknown/recovery-blocked` and another request is `conflict/operation-id-reused`.
6. If a stored answer exists under this `operation_id`, the same request digest answers the stored bytes, byte for byte, and another digest is `conflict/operation-id-reused`. This holds after later writes too: a replay after a `create` answers the stored bytes.
7. If any other intent is still blocked, the request is `operation-unknown/recovery-blocked`, before any content check.
8. The content check runs again under the lock, with the same two refusals as step 3. A target whose only entries are exempt goes on: the intent is written, `workbench.json` is written by temp file and rename, and the answer is stored and returned.

The plan function reads the directory under the lock and never the state the workbench was opened with, which recovery may have made stale. One content check serves steps 3 and 8.

**The reason names.** Two are new: `conflict/manifest-present` and `conflict/target-not-empty`. The detail of each names the first entries found, sorted. Every other refusal above already exists at `3c0903f4`. The fix of fusion issue `260930-1654_*_a-directory-named-workbench-json-makes-the-bundle-throw-and-exit-1-instead-of-answering.md` adds one diagnosis, `schema-invalid/manifest-not-a-file`: a `workbench.json` that is not a regular file is answered `unsupported` with that diagnosis on every operation, where the bundle throws and exits 1 today. None of the three names occurs under `codec/src`, `codec/contract`, `codec/schemas` or `codec/fixtures` at `3c0903f4`.

**The exemption.** One entry only: a `.json-state/` directory that holds nothing but what the lock protocol and the sweep own, with its `journal/` and `ops/` holding no named entry. The lock protocol owns the lock file, its takeover claims and the self-ignoring `.gitignore`. The sweep removes the dot-named entries of `journal/` and `ops/`. The names come from the codec's constants and are spelled once: `STATE_DIR`, `LOCK_FILE`, `TAKEOVER_INFIX` and `SELF_IGNORE` in `codec/src/store.ts`, and `JOURNAL_DIR` and `OPS_DIR` in `codec/src/journal.ts`. A stored answer of another operation in `ops/` is an entry, so such a target is `conflict/target-not-empty`. Scaffolding is not guessed at: `.DS_Store`, `.gitkeep`, a marker or an empty store directory is an entry like any other.

**The no-trace property.** A refused `initialize` over a directory without `.json-state/` leaves that directory byte-identical and creates no `.json-state/` in it, because the refusal comes from step 3, before the lock. A legacy store that FJ04 must migrate therefore gains nothing from a mistaken `initialize`.

### `list.result.state`

`list` answers `{workbench, state, scope, records}`. `state` is `json-control` or `legacy`, the same value `inspect`, `validate` and `reconcile` report. An `unsupported` workbench stays a refusal, as now. On a legacy workbench `records` stays `[]`. This is closure (a) of item 28, folded into this revision as `ad21e58` accepted. It does not waive the gate: a consumer that derives scope, work order or dispatch still calls `inspect` first and proceeds only on `json-control`.

### `inspect`: fifteen operations, and `pending`

`inspect.result.operations.implemented` gains `initialize` after `validate`. The table then holds fifteen operations: fourteen implemented, with `migration` still deferred. At `3c0903f4` `OPERATIONS` in `codec/src/cli/protocol.ts` holds fourteen, and thirteen of them are implemented.

`inspect` gains one additive field, `pending`. It is `null`, or `{operation_id, blocked}`. It is non-null exactly when the state is `legacy` and the target's only entry is a `.json-state/` holding a committed `initialize` intent, with the same exemption as above. It is computed by the same content check that `initialize` runs, so the exemption list stays in the codec. Item 33 states why.

### The `reconcile` index: placement and preserved orderings

This change is for speed alone and moves no answer byte. At `3c0903f4` `readContext` in `codec/src/kernel.ts` resolves a record id by walking `controlFiles(wb, wb.root)` and strict-parsing every control file, on each call. `reconcile` calls it per reference site, evidence binding and dependency edge. Fusion issue `260930-1712_*_the-codecs-reconcile-grows-with-the-square-of-the-record-count-and-outlasts-the-clients-timeout-from-about-1200-records.md` measured an unscoped `reconcile` at 2.1 s for 200 records, 11.8 s for 500, 47.1 s for 1 000 and 310 s for 2 500. Those are the issue's figures, taken on an M2 Max and not re-run for this text.

- **Placement.** The index is built inside `reconcile`'s body and nowhere else. The body wraps the read context it already builds, and the wrapper's `resolveRecordId` answers from one walk. `readContext`, its `resolveRecordId`, and every mutation (the free-id check of `create` included) stay as they are. A cached index inside a mutation could go stale within that mutation.
- **Lifetime.** One index per run of the body. `read` runs the body again after a consistency retry or a recovery, so the index is rebuilt with the view. Nothing is persisted, and no index outlives a request.
- **Preserved orderings and outcomes.** The walk is `controlFiles` in its existing sorted order. Every file is strict-parsed, and a refused file is skipped, so it stays unresolvable. No `blockedOn` filter applies, as today. The detail of `conflict/ambiguous-reference` lists the hits in walk order. Missing and foreign targets and blocked-recovery findings are unchanged. Every answer byte of every recorded session and of the handback stays as it is.

### The role in `reconcile`'s references

A reference entry becomes `{path, at, role?, status, target?, class?, reason?}`.

- **Applicability.** Every entry whose `at` is `/active_documents/<i>/ref` carries `role`, whatever its `status` (`resolved`, `unresolved`, `ambiguous` or `foreign`). Every other entry carries none.
- **Value.** The stored `active_documents[i].role` of the record at `path`, when that is `plan` or `spec`. It is left out when the stored value is anything else, a case the record's schema finding already reports. The role is taken from the binding at its source, never inferred from the target's kind, file name or array position. Both roles can name plan-kind records.
- **Placement.** Immediately after `at` and before `status` in the entry's key order, which the answer keeps as written (the recorded entries read `path`, `at`, `status`, then `target` or `class` and `reason`).
- **Unchanged.** `path`, `at`, `status`, `target`, `class`, `reason` and revision semantics. An unresolved or ambiguous binding carries `role` and no `target`, so nothing is invented about a target that did not resolve.

On the fusion side, `bin/fusion-citation-sweep` then takes each binding's role from this report and sends no `show` per package or evidence row. Its request count becomes exactly `inspect`, `list` and `reconcile` for any number of packages.

### The FJ02 delta file

`codec/fixtures/protocol-session-fj02/15-reconcile.response.json` holds ten reference entries. Two of them are at `/active_documents/0/ref`: `work-packages/260927-0900-strict-reader/package.json`, `unresolved` (`record-not-found`), and `work-packages/260929-0900-fj02-session/package.json`, `resolved`. The stored role of both bindings is `plan` (the fixture workbench's package, and the answer of exchange 07). The role therefore changes exactly those two entries, each by `"role":"plan"` after `at`.

The historical file is not edited. Beside it, a new file, `codec/fixtures/protocol-session-fj02/15-reconcile.role-delta.json`, names the fields the new answer adds, by entry path and pointer. The FJ02 gate replays exchange 15 against the recorded bytes with exactly that delta applied, and every other exchange byte for byte. It is shown red against an answer that carries one field more or one less. The session's `README.md` states the delta and why. Nothing is regenerated silently. The evidence is kept in two stages, as `a15dfc8` asks. The index lands first, and every recorded session and the handback replay byte for byte without the delta. Only then does the role move the two reviewed fields.

### The measurement protocol

Kept apart from the deterministic tests, as `a15dfc8` asks.

- **Deterministic work count** (in the codec suite). A fixture of N packages, each carrying several references, is reconciled at N and 2N. The test counts whole-store walks and control-file parses in one `reconcile`. Parses at 2N may be at most twice the figure at N plus a constant: work grows with records plus references, not their product. The code at `3c0903f4` fails it. A second test pins the rebuild on the retry path: a record lands during the first read attempt and the answer resolves it. Timing is not asserted in the suite.
- **Timing at scale** (outside the suite). A generator, `codec/scripts/bench-fixture.mjs`, is committed so that Prior can rebuild the fixtures. It builds the store through the bundle with fixed ids, as the issue did: packages each with one adopted plan, at 200, 500, 1 000 and 2 500 records. Each run goes through the committed bundle with an empty environment. The hand-over reports, per size: records and reference count, machine and Node version, at least three repetitions, median and maximum elapsed time, peak memory (max RSS), and the output bytes of unscoped `reconcile` and unscoped `list`.
- **The consumers at scale.** At 2 500 records, in a `git init` project, the complete helpers: `bin/fusion-claimed-package`, `bin/fusion-work-order`, `bin/fusion-citation-check` and `bin/fusion-citation-sweep --dry-run`. A fast lookup in isolation is not taken as proof that they finish in time.
- **The targets.** An unscoped `reconcile` at 2 500 records answers within the 5 s post-wait allowance (`POST_WAIT_MARGIN_MS` in `hooks/lib/record-client.ts`) on the reference machine, an M2 Max. Each consumer exits 0 within the client's 70 s (`CODEC_WAIT_MS` 65 s plus that margin), with its time recorded. If `reconcile` misses the target, the remaining cost is located and fixed before the hand-over. The timeout is never raised. Output bytes are stated against 1 MiB, and an answer above it is reported for item 31. It does not stop this revision, and no migration readiness is claimed.

### 33. No read finishes a committed `initialize` whose manifest has not landed; `inspect` names that window

**Closes:** fusion decision `260930-1654_*_does-a-read-finish-a-committed-initialize-whose-manifest-has-not-landed-or-report-the-target-as-legacy.md`, option 3, answered by the user on 2026-09-30 together with the plan's approval. This is fusion's decision, stated openly for Prior to object to. Prior has not co-decided it, and `a15dfc8` says that choosing the bundle grouping does not decide it. Preferred form: an objection before the hand-over that freezes this revision's digest, or none.

The window: `initialize` is the first operation whose intent can stand in a workbench that has no manifest. Between its commit point (the intent directory renamed into `.json-state/journal/`) and the write of `workbench.json`, the target holds `.json-state/` and nothing else. At `3c0903f4`, `openWorkbench` reports such a target `legacy`, because the state is a function of the manifest alone. `read` in `codec/src/kernel.ts` runs a read on every state but `json-control` once, without recovery (its first line). `inspect` runs outside `read` and recovers nothing.

> **33. No read finishes a committed `initialize` whose manifest has not landed; `inspect` names that window.** The target then holds only `.json-state/` and is `legacy` under section 4.1. Not finishing is inside section 6's permission ("dürfen", "kann"). The statement is about this intent alone: ordinary committed intents are still recovered by FJ02 reads, as `a15dfc8` requires. Any `initialize` request finishes it under the lock. The same request answers the committed result; another one lands the committed intent first and is then refused `conflict/manifest-present`. `inspect` gains one additive field, `pending`. It is non-null exactly when the state is `legacy` and the only entry is a `.json-state/` holding a committed `initialize`, and it names that intent's operation id and whether it is blocked. **A duty on Prior's host:** its provisioning route acts on what `inspect` reports for that window (it sends `initialize`, never FJ04) and does not re-derive the codec's exemption list.

The permission cited: section 6 at `a15dfc8`, the `inspect`, `list`, `show`, `validate` row ("eindeutige bereits committete Intents dürfen zuvor rekonstruiert werden"), and the paragraph on `recovery-blocked` ("Lesen kann einen committeten Intent fertigstellen"). Section 4.1 adds that reading creates no new initialisation ("Lesen erzeugt weiterhin keinen neuen Initialisierungsauftrag"), and it leaves open who reconstructs the committed initialisation bytes after an abort. The decision answers that: an `initialize` request does.

Why `pending`, and not `legacy` alone: without a report from the codec, each host would have to rebuild the exemption list to tell a pending initialisation apart from a legacy store bound for FJ04. Two copies of that list would drift. With `pending`, the list stays in one place, and Setup and Prior's provisioning route each read one field. This is inferred, not measured.

The two alternatives the record weighed, for Prior's review. In option 1, reads report `legacy` with no field, so a caller has to look for `.json-state/` itself. In option 2, every read, `inspect` included, finishes the intent. That would make the gate both hosts call first into a read that may write, on a legacy-state target too.

What it fixes in the bytes both hosts replay: the `initialize` recorded session, planned for the hand-over. There, `inspect` over a committed intent from an in-process cut answers `legacy` with `pending`. The intent's own `initialize` request then answers the committed result, and a following `inspect` answers `json-control`. The flowchart under "The order" does not depend on this item. If Prior objects, `read`, `inspect` and those exchanges change, and the order stays as stated.

### What landed

1. **`initialize` (request 27)** (`639d6185`). Request, manifest, answer, the eight steps of "The order", the reason names, the exemption and the no-trace property are as the section above states them, with one widening Prior accepted at `7909838`. The exemption also covers the temp files the lock protocol writes for the lock, a takeover claim and the self-ignore (`lockProtocolOwns` in `codec/src/store.ts`, over the protocol's own temp-name grammar). A waiter's temp file stands in `.json-state/` while another process holds the lock, and without the widening the winner of a race would be refused `target-not-empty`. The self-ignore is exempt only while it holds exactly `*`. There is no generic `.tmp` exemption, and nothing deletes a competitor's file. The race evidence Prior asked for is in the suite. `codec/src/__tests__/kernel.test.ts`, `describe("initialize through the kernel")`, has two cases: "two initializers in one process, the first paused holding the lock: exactly one lands, the manifest id is the winner's, the other is manifest-present", and "two bundle processes at once over one empty directory: exactly one lands and the manifest id is the winner's". `codec/src/__tests__/store.test.ts`, `describe("lockProtocolOwns: …")`, admits the protocol's names and refuses a self-ignore with other bytes, a stranger's temp file and any other name. Two properties the section above did not name. A refusal before the lock stands only while `.json-state` is still no directory after the check; otherwise the lock path answers, so a concurrent initializer's work in flight is never read as `target-not-empty`. The presence of `workbench.json` is read with `lstat`, so a dangling link is `manifest-not-a-file`, not an absent manifest.
2. **The reason names, as pinned.** Five are new with this revision. Every other refusal reuses a reason FJ02 already answered.

   | Reason | Answered by | Pinned by |
   |---|---|---|
   | `conflict/manifest-present` | a fresh `initialize` over a `workbench.json` entry of any kind | session `10-initialize`; `ops.test.ts` `describe("initialize")`; the two race cases |
   | `conflict/target-not-empty` | a fresh `initialize` over any other entry | session `04-initialize`, the target byte-identical after it; the installed-copy case (item 8) |
   | `schema-invalid/manifest-not-a-file` | a `workbench.json` that is not a regular file, in the four envelopes of item 4 | session `24-inspect`; `store.test.ts`; `ops.test.ts` ("a directory named workbench.json: …") |
   | `operation-unknown/pending-initialize-unreadable` | `inspect` over journal data it cannot read, item 4 | session `26-inspect`; `ops.test.ts` `describe("inspect.pending and list.state")` |
   | `operation-unknown/pending-initialize-ambiguous` | `inspect` over more than one committed `initialize` | `ops.test.ts`, same `describe`; no session exchange |

   The kernel's state gate passes the diagnosis on as `unsupported-format/manifest-not-a-file` to every other mutation. `unsupported-format` is FJ02's class, and the reason is the diagnosis's.
3. **`list.result.state` (item 28, closure (a))** (`639d6185`). `list` answers `{workbench, state, scope, records}`, as the section above states. On the Claude side (`bc3b04a8`) one function reads it: `listedRecords` in `hooks/lib/codec-read.ts`. `hooks/lib/scope.ts`, `hooks/lib/work-graph.ts` and `hooks/lib/record-index.ts` call it after their gate, which stays first and unchanged. `json-control` hands on `records`. `legacy` means the manifest was lost between the gate and `list`, and is read as `legacy`. Anything else, an absent `state` included, is `unanswered/unparseable` on `list`. So the gate-to-`list` window item 28 described is closed on this side.
4. **`inspect`: fifteen operations, and `pending` as corrected** (`639d6185`, `fb9e1b10`). `operations.implemented` holds fourteen names, `initialize` after `validate`, with `migration` deferred. The section above is corrected here, not edited, in the three points `ae1ad78` `## 33` named:
   - **The shape.** `pending` is `null | {operation_id, id, blocked}`. `id` is the workbench UUID read from the staged manifest after strict parse and the workbench schema, and it must equal the id in the intent's recorded answer. A host that lost its request rebuilds it as `{op: "initialize", workbench: <target>, operation_id, id}` from the target and this field alone.
   - **Detection.** Detection is independent of the fresh-target exemption. `pending` names every committed `initialize` in `.json-state/journal/` until its intent leaves the journal, whatever else the root holds and whatever `workbench.json` is (absent, other bytes, not a file). That includes the cuts after the manifest's write, where `state` is already `json-control`. `state` stays the one the manifest's bytes give, and `pending` grants nothing. `blocked` is the kernel's recovery classification, `blockedIntent` in `codec/src/kernel.ts`, exported for this purpose and called by the lock-free classification too.
   - **Unreadable data.** Journal data `inspect` cannot read is never `pending: null`. Four cases are refused `operation-unknown/pending-initialize-unreadable`: a committed entry that does not read, an `initialize` whose writes are not exactly `workbench.json`, a staged manifest that does not validate, and one whose id differs from the recorded answer (or an answer that names none). More than one committed `initialize` is `operation-unknown/pending-initialize-ambiguous`. Each detail names the intent directories.

   **The four envelopes** replace "on every operation" in the section above's reason names. `inspect` answers `ok` with `state: unsupported`, the diagnosis `schema-invalid/manifest-not-a-file`, and `pending` when one stands. `list`, `show`, `validate` and `reconcile` refuse with the diagnosis itself. Every mutation but `initialize` is refused by the state gate, `unsupported-format/manifest-not-a-file`. On `initialize`, a replay under its id (the stored answer or `conflict/operation-id-reused`) and a blocked intent (`operation-unknown/recovery-blocked`) keep their precedence, and only a fresh request reaches `conflict/manifest-present`. No code change was needed for the precedence; `ops.test.ts` ("precedence on initialize: …") now pins it.

   **One consequence beyond `initialize`.** A committed entry that does not read is refused whatever the state, since its `op` cannot be known. `inspect` over a JSON-controlled workbench whose journal holds such an entry therefore now refuses `pending-initialize-unreadable`, where it answered `json-control` before. The other reads refuse `conflict/journal-unreadable`, as they did. On the Claude side the stop moves from `list` to the gate. Each FJ03a and FJ03b helper still refuses by name with its exit code for a read the codec refused, which is the same for a refused gate and a refused `list`. Observed and left as it is: for an `intent.json` that does not parse, the detail names the intent directory twice (session `26-inspect`), because the kernel's own detail already begins with it.
5. **The `reconcile` index** (`de3a135c`). It is as the section above states: built inside `reconcile`'s body, one per read attempt, rebuilt after a consistency retry or a recovery, never reaching a mutation. The work count at N = 8 and 2N = 16 packages, each with four references and one edge, was taken before (at `639d6185`) and after. Whole-store walks went from 56 and 104 to 3 and 3. Strict parses went from 636 and 2 011 to 53 and 92. `ops.test.ts` asserts that parses at 2N are at most twice those at N plus 3, and that walks are equal. The retry case lands a `create` during the first attempt, and the second attempt resolves it. A comparison case holds the index to the kernel's resolver on found, missing, blocked and three-way ambiguous ids with a refused copy present. The comment above `readContext` in `kernel.ts` now names the body-local index (`fb9e1b10`).
6. **The role in `reconcile`'s references** (`2146eefa`). It is as the section above states. `role` follows `at` on every `/active_documents/<i>/ref` entry, whatever its status, and it is copied from the binding at its source. It stands on no other entry. `ops.test.ts` ("the role: …") covers a plan-role and a spec-role binding of plan-kind records, an unresolved and an ambiguous binding with `role` and no `target`, and a non-binding reference without `role`.
7. **The sweep reads the role, and sends no `show`** (`f3923b23`). `bin/fusion-citation-sweep` takes each package binding from `reconcile`'s references, and each evidence record's report from the naming rule. A spawned count test logs every request: exactly `inspect`, `list` and `reconcile`, where the code at `2146eefa` sent three `show` after them. The dry run over 40 packages of FJ03b step 4, rebuilt through the bundle, took 11.6 to 15.0 s over six runs before the change and 1.18 to 1.38 s after, with identical stdout. This removes the per-row cost the FJ03b section's "No request for a binding field in `list`" named, as that section said fusion would.
8. **The installed copy** (`15cff61d`). A fourth case in `codec/src/__tests__/install.test.ts`. The installed `bin/fusion-record` initialises an empty `fusion-workbench/` in a `git init` project. The answer is the ruled shape, and the manifest parses to the ruled five fields. With the marker then written, as Setup would write it, `inspect` answers `json-control` with the request's id and `pending: null`. `bin/fusion-claimed-package` exits 0 with nothing printed, and `bin/fusion-work-order` prints `verdict=empty`. A v12 workbench is refused `conflict/target-not-empty`, the detail naming `.fusion-setup, work-packages`. Its tree is byte-identical after, with no `.json-state/`.
9. **What did not change.** The lock, the journal and its format, the read protocol, and the recovery of every intent but a committed `initialize` by reads are the files and behaviour the FJ02 section states. `mutate` still admits only `json-control` for every operation but `initialize`. `create`'s free-id check still resolves through `readContext`. No fixture that existed under `codec/fixtures/valid/`, `invalid/` or `bytes/` at `b3909330` changed; the manifest gained five entries, and one existing note says "fifteen" where it said "fourteen". The Claude side sends the same four operations as before (`inspect`, `list`, `show`, `reconcile`) and no mutation; `initialize` has no Claude-side caller until FJ03c and FJ03d.

### The evidence, kept apart

**Step 4, byte identity.** At a scratch commit carrying exactly step 4's files, the six FJ01 pairs, the handback, the fifteen FJ02 exchanges (`15-reconcile` included, with no delta) and the twenty FJ02b exchanges replayed byte for byte, and nothing was regenerated. That is the performance-only evidence `a15dfc8` asks for, taken before the role moved a byte.

**Step 5, the reviewed delta.** `15-reconcile.response.json` is not edited. `15-reconcile.role-delta.json` beside it names two adds in `/result/references`: `"role": "plan"` after `at` on the two `/active_documents/0/ref` entries, one `unresolved` and one `resolved`, as the section above named them. The FJ02 gate replays exchange 15 against the recorded bytes with exactly those two fields added, and every other exchange byte for byte. It checks that the delta names every binding of the recorded answer and nothing else, and it is red against an answer with one field more or one less. `UPDATE_PROTOCOL_SESSION_FJ02=1` never rewrites exchange 15.

The subsets `records-<N>/` are the manifest and the first N/2 packages, whole, without `.json-state/`. Run as `node codec/scripts/bench-fixture.mjs <out-dir> [--sizes 200,500,1000,2500]`.
